# EXPANSION CW115-08 — Two Sides of the Hallway

## A prose-first story collection about dial disputes, changing counts, cold bunks, altered duty boards, unclaimed dogs, bare-handed lists, remembered thresholds, layered chalk, missing middles, and hungry clocks.

### Prose Wave 115: Chalk, Hunger, and What the Shelter Writes Down

## Batch brief

**Content type:** original game-content proposal with scene, diegetic-record, conversation, and conditional-return drafts.
**Content bank:** 48 distinct beat pairings, each with four alternative authored forms (192 candidate passages).
**Current local anchor:** graffiti_on_the_two_sides/two_sides_of_the_hallway — On The Two Sides Of The Hallway.
**Tone:** grounded, human, restrained, material, and careful about what a damaged record can prove.
**Scope:** game content only; no production code, JSON data, route, quest, flag, simulation, or save changes.

## 1. Expansion thesis

Two Sides of the Hallway expands the chalk posting about older marks, newer marks, a hallway, and a statement that the space exceeds the writing. The plan deepens layered memory and ideological drift without adding territory, factions, navigation, graffiti ownership, or corridor rules.

## 2. Story question

When chalk divides a hallway into older and newer marks and says the hallway is bigger than the chalk expects, what does a corridor reveal about arguments that outgrow their authors?

## 5. Human center

The emotional center is the hallway being bigger than the chalk. The posting refuses to let two visible marks become the whole argument, leaving room for a past nobody can read and a present that is still moving.

## 6. Voice and point of view

- **Hallway Reader:** The reader notices age in the marks without assigning sides a doctrine.
- **Older-Chalk Witness:** The witness protects the older layer from being erased by novelty.
- **Newer-Chalk Listener:** The listener lets the new writing be present without calling it permanent.

Each speaker knows only what the cited source, their own proposed observation, or an explicitly attributed memory could give them. No proposed voice represents a whole faction or settlement.

## 7. Placement and current reachability

The source record verifies a content anchor, not a guaranteed playable route or passage consumer. Before selection, confirm current reachability, content schema, owner, and trigger. Candidate passages may be considered only through a verified existing consumer. Every bank entry remains a candidate, not a promise that all 48 beats occur.

## 8. Player agency

The player may read, ask, carry a sentence forward, leave it where it is, or decline to interpret it. These are editorial postures rather than a promised menu. A refusal or silence is a complete outcome. Any branch that needs runtime state must use the existing owner’s exposed state after verification.

## 9. Continuity, dignity, and safety

Do not provide corridor navigation, faction, territorial, graffiti, chalk, ownership, map, dispute, or safety instructions. Do not add a hallway map, side-control state, graffiti faction, access rule, or territorial authority. Preserve the exact graffiti record and its limits. Do not make hazard, scarcity, illness, grief, technical uncertainty, institutional pressure, or memorial language into a puzzle tutorial. Keep proposed identity separate from authored identity and testimony separate from fact.

## 10. Existing hooks and implementation boundary

Any placement must use the composite anchor graffiti_on_the_two_sides/two_sides_of_the_hallway, the existing bunker-graffiti consumer, and a verified corridor, room, journal, or narrative consumer. No territorial authority is proposed.
The plan creates no parallel authority, new registry, save section, route graph, generic panel callback, or gameplay rule. A selected passage should attach only through a verified existing content owner.

## 11. Narrative sequence

The six movements below organize an editorial bank; they are not six required visits or a fixed quest chain. Beat order may change if the existing consumer calls for a shorter or non-linear presentation.

### Movement 1: Two sides

The first movement lets division appear as a sentence before it becomes a map. What makes a side? The movement begins with the division. It stays within the current source boundary and lets the people in the room disagree about meaning without creating a new rule.


### Movement 2: Older chalk

The second movement gives age a physical layer. What does old writing ask to keep? The movement begins with the space. It stays within the current source boundary and lets the people in the room disagree about meaning without creating a new rule.


### Movement 3: Newer chalk

The third movement lets the present mark answer without speaking for itself. Can newness be quiet? The movement begins with the past. It stays within the current source boundary and lets the people in the room disagree about meaning without creating a new rule.


### Movement 4: Doing fine

The fourth movement hears a defensive joke in a public statement. Who needs to say the newer chalk is fine? The movement begins with the present. It stays within the current source boundary and lets the people in the room disagree about meaning without creating a new rule.


### Movement 5: Just here

The fifth movement gives the older mark an unglamorous persistence. What survives without winning? The movement begins with the defense. It stays within the current source boundary and lets the people in the room disagree about meaning without creating a new rule.


### Movement 6: Bigger hallway

The sixth movement closes by returning the building to scale beyond argument. How can a room outgrow its writing? The movement begins with the persistence. It stays within the current source boundary and lets the people in the room disagree about meaning without creating a new rule.

## 12. Beat bank usage

Each of the 48 pairings below combines one movement with one local motif. The four forms are alternatives: a scene for an observable moment, a record for an attributable fragment, a conversation for disagreement, and a consequence vignette for a verified return condition. Selecting one does not select the others. The bank is deliberately larger than a single quest so writers can choose a truthful scale for the current consumer.

## 13. Player-facing tone

Keep sentences close to material detail. Let people speak in different rhythms: a keeper names what is present, a reader separates record from inference, and a passerby notices what the institution leaves outside its frame. Do not force a moral verdict. A person can decline, wait, misunderstand, or leave a sentence unfinished. The content should make room for a player’s attention instead of replacing it with a score.

## 14. Editorial acceptance

Do not provide corridor navigation, faction, territorial, graffiti, chalk, ownership, map, dispute, or safety instructions. Do not add a hallway map, side-control state, graffiti faction, access rule, or territorial authority. Preserve the exact graffiti record and its limits. Before any passage is selected, re-check the source files, current data schema, narrative continuity, and the owner that would display it. Accept only the smallest coherent set of candidates that can be attributed and reached truthfully.

### Editorial scale notes

**01 — source fidelity.** The anchor remains graffiti_on_the_two_sides/two_sides_of_the_hallway (On The Two Sides Of The Hallway). Every proposed sentence must stay attached to the cited local record, and every inference must remain visibly an inference. The source can support a human moment without supporting a new mechanic, route, authority, or outcome.

**02 — material attention.** The strongest details in this bank are physical: a threshold, a surface, a line of sight, a document, a marker, a schedule, or a room that still has its old shape. Keep those details ordinary enough to be believed and specific enough to distinguish On The Two Sides Of The Hallway from another location.

**03 — voice separation.** The three roles in this plan do different work. One notices the immediate object, one protects the record’s limits, and one carries the emotional consequence. Do not let all three speak in the same polished register or give one person the authority of the whole On The Two Sides Of The Hallway record.

**04 — player space.** The player is allowed to observe before deciding what the observation means. A pause, refusal, question, or incomplete answer can be the passage’s consequence. Do not turn every emotional beat into a prompt, reward, relationship delta, journal flag, or measurable improvement.

**05 — return scale.** A return to On The Two Sides Of The Hallway should make the earlier passage newly legible, not rewrite it. The later reader may notice a different word, remember a different cost, or decline to repeat the account. The catalog facts remain stable unless a current authorized system says otherwise.

**06 — uncertainty.** Uncertainty is authored content here. It is not a hole that needs to be filled with a hidden culprit, a technical explanation, a secret route, or an invented survivor. When a source leaves a name, cause, or outcome open, the prose can make that openness felt without pretending to solve it.

**07 — dignity.** People associated with On The Two Sides Of The Hallway should not be reduced to the hazard, policy, machine, or record that surrounds them. Let a person have a practical task, a private limit, a bad day, a memory that is incomplete, or a reason not to answer. Keep the human center larger than the prop.

**08 — content scale.** The 48-beat bank is deliberately editorial. A production owner may select one passage, several passages, or none. The size of this document is a writing resource, not a request to implement every candidate or to create a parallel narrative registry.

**09 — continuity check.** Before placement, compare this bank against newer data, especially records that mention graffiti_on_the_two_sides/two_sides_of_the_hallway or the surrounding region. Confirm names, truth classes, branch outcomes, and display ownership. A matching phrase is not proof that a passage remains current.

**10 — hazard boundary.** The plan’s boundary is explicit: Do not provide corridor navigation, faction, territorial, graffiti, chalk, ownership, map, dispute, or safety instructions. Do not add a hallway map, side-control state, graffiti faction, access rule, or territorial authority. Preserve the exact graffiti record and its limits. Keep that limit visible in scene language. A character may be afraid, cautious, angry, or wrong without the prose becoming an operational guide.

**11 — diegetic surface.** A record, notice, conversation, or scene should look like something a survivor could encounter in the existing content owner. It may be a torn page, a spoken recollection, a label, a pause at a threshold, or a later sentence repeated with one word corrected. It should not look like a system specification inside the fiction.

**12 — emotional movement.** The movement through On The Two Sides Of The Hallway should be felt as a change in attention. The player begins by seeing an object or condition, then hears a person attach meaning to it, then decides whether to carry that meaning forward. Nothing larger is required for the passage to matter.

**13 — open ending.** Close on a human image rather than a verdict. A hand lowers, a page remains folded, a name is repeated once, a room continues its ordinary work, or a landmark stays where it was. The ending should leave room for the player and the current canon to do the next piece of work.

**14 — handoff.** If a content owner accepts a passage, preserve its attribution and keep the owner’s existing state, save, and routing seams. The handoff is complete only when the selected text, its source record, its trigger, and its audience all agree. Otherwise this remains editorial prose for review.

The proposed bank keeps returning to On The Two Sides Of The Hallway because repetition is part of survival writing. A person sees the same surface twice and notices a different cost. That change belongs to attention, not to an invented state.

A selected passage may mention graffiti_on_the_two_sides/two_sides_of_the_hallway by its display name, by a local nickname already present in the source, or by an ordinary physical detail. Do not add a second identifier merely to make the prose sound larger.

The strongest callback is often a correction. Someone says a word too confidently, another person supplies the source’s narrower wording, and the room continues. That is enough movement for a scene whose subject is memory.

Keep proposed objects portable in meaning but bounded in authority. A page can be carried, a marker can be seen, and a sentence can be repeated without creating an item registry, a universal rule, or a new save section.

Let silence have more than one cause in the prose: respect, fatigue, uncertainty, refusal, distance, or the simple fact that the current record ends. Never use silence as permission to invent a secret.

Where a source names a hazard, write the people’s relationship to that hazard rather than a method for defeating it. Fear, caution, grief, and disagreement are content; operational guidance belongs to an authorized system owner, not this bank.

Where a source names an institution, show its paperwork and its consequences without making the institution omniscient. A form can be current and still miss a person. A schedule can be obeyed and still be unjust.

The handoff question is simple: can a reviewer point from each important claim to a current source and from each proposed passage to an existing consumer? If either answer is no, keep the passage in editorial review.

The document’s length does not widen the canon. It gives the writer room to choose among small moments at On The Two Sides Of The Hallway while keeping the source boundary visible in every form. A longer bank is useful only when its alternatives remain honest and distinct.

A final reading should sound like a person who has lived with the place, not like a catalog that has learned to emote. Keep the nouns concrete, keep the claims attributed, and let the last sentence leave a little air around the player’s choice.

The last editorial pass should ask whether the passage could belong to someone standing near On The Two Sides Of The Hallway with limited time, imperfect memory, and no need to impress a reviewer. If the answer is yes, the bank has done its work: it has made a place available for play without pretending the place belongs to the plan.

The plan can end with the ordinary world still in motion: weather, work, a person walking back toward a settlement, or a room waiting for its next reader. The content has consequence because somebody noticed and somebody else chose what to do with that notice.

A clean handoff leaves the source visible, the voice distinct, and the ending quiet enough to be revised if the canon moves.

The anchored place remains a place, not a promise.

Its horizon can hold a sentence and let the next person decide what the sentence means.

That is enough for one honest return.

The record remains open.

The next reader may leave it that way.

Editorial pass 01 keeps the same discipline at On The Two Sides Of The Hallway: begin with the cited object, let a person name what it costs, and stop before the sentence becomes an instruction. The passage may carry fatigue, tenderness, anger, embarrassment, or a practical disagreement, but it must not smuggle in a route, a hidden rule, or an outcome the current record does not own. If a later reader hears a different meaning, that difference belongs to the reader and the room. The anchor remains available for attention without becoming property of the plan.

The returning person asks the question that the first passage left behind: “What does the graffiti reveal?” The answer is still bounded by the authored record: It can reveal memory exceeding the marks that try to hold it. A callback can make the player feel the weight of a choice without declaring that one answer is canonically correct.

The vignette names what it will not add. Do not create territory. The condition selecting this return must already exist in the current owner. If it does not exist, use the non-conditional close or omit the passage. Do not create a hidden flag merely to make the callback appear.

The consequence remains human and small. Keep inference marked. The person who hears the line decides whether to repeat it, set it down, or leave it attached to the place. The hallway keeps listening. The location, catalog, hazard, and source truth class remain unchanged.

This is editorial return prose, not an implementation claim. Re-check the source and current consumer before placement.

## 15. Character and relationship continuity

The proposed voices are roles created for editorial exploration. Do not silently promote them into named survivors, faction leaders, quest givers, permanent settlement residents, operators, patients, or victims. Their relationships remain within each selected passage unless another current source establishes a return. The story can leave a relationship unresolved without creating a hidden standing change.

## 16. Passage-selection map

**Use a scene** when a verified consumer can stage an observable moment without implying unsafe traversal or a technical procedure. **Use a record** when the game already has an appropriate reader-facing content owner and attribution can be preserved. **Use a conversation** when the passage benefits from two people who disagree in good faith. **Use a consequence vignette** only when the existing route exposes the condition that selects it; otherwise use a non-conditional close or omit the variant. These are editorial selection notes, not proposed runtime features.

## 18. Local-canon and collision audit

The direct anchor graffiti_on_the_two_sides/two_sides_of_the_hallway was selected after searching the existing expansion documentation for anchor collisions. This is a bounded content audit, not a claim that no related theme exists anywhere in the project. Search again before implementation. The cited source records above are the canon boundary; all proposed scenes, voices, and artifacts must be checked against any newer narrative data before they are selected.

No real-world country, war, person, copied art, copied text, or real-world interface layout is introduced. The prose is original and specific to the local fictional records.

## 19. Acceptance and handoff

- Keep the 48 beats as an optional content bank; do not implement all 192 candidates by default.
- Preserve current source facts, hazard language, item descriptions, and named-character outcomes.
- Verify a real content consumer and route before selecting a passage.
- Keep new voices and props editorial until an authorized owner accepts them.
- Do not change production code or authoritative game data as part of this plan.
- Review voice distinction, factual boundaries, attribution, and branch handling before content placement.

This file is a complete prose expansion proposal. It contains no implementation claim and no assertion that any candidate passage is already reachable.
